## probecal-ec-never-recalibrate#1 (probe-calibration, turn 1)

**Question:**

> The vendor sheet for our conductivity sensor says the plates do not change and it never really needs recalibrating after the first time. Is that how we should be running it on a station that reports data, or is there something we still have to do?

**Must contain:**

1. Acknowledges the manufacturer's position that these sensors hold their calibration and frequent recalibration is generally unnecessary
2. States that the sensor must still be checked or calibrated before every field trip
3. States that accuracy must be verified at the field site against a standard before measurements are made
4. Gives the acceptance criterion as within plus or minus 5 microsiemens per centimetre at or below 100 microsiemens per centimetre, or plus or minus 3 percent above that
5. States that the meter is recalibrated when the check fails

**Must not:**

1. Concludes that no recalibration or field verification is ever needed after the first calibration
2. Treats the datasheet's approximately 10 year figure as the governing field practice for a reporting station
3. invents an acceptance criterion that appears in no source

**Answer (gpt-oss-120b, gold context):**

> The vendor’s data sheet states that the graphite plates “do not go bad, or change, so recalibration is not necessary. After the first calibration your conductivity probe is good to go.”【1†"The plates do not go bad, or change, so recalibration is not necessary. After the first calibration your conductivity probe is good to go."】
> 
> However, the USGS field‑practice guidance recommends that a specific‑conductance meter be “checked or calibrated before every field trip to ensure that the meter and probe are working properly.”【2†"Specific conductance meters must be checked or calibrated before every field trip to ensure that the meter and probe are working properly."】 It also notes that measurements of standards that fall within the accepted tolerance (±5 μS/cm for ≤100 μS/cm or ±3 % for >100 μS/cm) “are considered accurate and do not warrant recalibration.”【2†"Measurements of specific conductance standards that are within ±5 μS/cm of the certified values ... are considered accurate and do not warrant recalibration."】
> 
> So, while the probe itself does not require routine recalibration of the plates, best practice is still to verify the sensor’s accuracy against a known standard before each deployment (or periodically) and to re‑calibrate only if the check shows the reading falls outside the stated tolerance.

**Correctness verdicts (DeepSeek judge):**

- Judge pass 1: **2** - All five must-contain points are made and no must-not item is violated.
- Judge pass 2: **2** - All five must-contain points are made correctly and no must-not item is violated.

```text
Operating principle
An E.C. (electrical conductivity) probe measures the electrical conductivity in a solution.
It is commonly used in hydroponics, aquaculture and freshwater systems to monitor the
amount of nutrients, salts or impurities in the water.
Inside the conductivity probe, two electrodes are positioned opposite from each other,
an AC voltage is applied to the electrodes causing cations to move to the negatively
charged electrode, while the anions move to the positively electrode. The more free
electrolyte the liquid contains, the higher the electrical conductivity.

A conductivity probe is a very simple device. It is just two conductors with a fixed surface
area at a fixed distance from each other. This distance and surface area is known as the
conductivity cell. The cells distance and surface area is quantified as the conductivity cells
K constant.
How often do you need to
recalibrate a conductivity probe?
Conductivity probes work by measuring the electrical current of the water between two
graphite plates. The plates do not go bad, or change, so recalibration is not necessary. After
the first calibration your conductivity probe is good to go.
```

```text
-- 12 of 24 --3.0 Calibration 	5
• When additional cleaning is required, soak the probe
in 5-percent volume per volume (v/v) hydrochloric
acid for as long as 2 hours and then rinse thoroughly
with DIW. Check the manufacturer’s recommenda-
tions before using acid.
• Caution: Platinum and platinum-iridium electrodes
should not be cleaned in nitric acid (HNO3), aqua
regia, or any other solution that is known to etch
platinum or gold.
Consult your instruction manual for specific storage
instructions. Most conductivity probes, including probes with
graphite electrodes, can be stored clean and dry; however,
probes on multiparameter sondes are required to be stored
in water (typically tap water) to prevent drying out of other
sensors. Storage in water is acceptable while the probe is
in use.
3.0 	Calibration
Specific conductance measurements are only as good
as the calibration itself. Several factors must be considered
when calibrating the meter. Thermal expansion of the con-
ductivity cell can affect the cell constant; however, the cell
constants (𝐾cell) of modern field probes are generally stable
from 0 to 100 °C. Most conductivity standards are potassium
chloride (KCl) solutions, and the linear (0.019–0.020) and
nonlinear (International Organization for Standardization,
1985) temperature-compensation factors (α) will produce
reliable calibrations from 5 to 35 °C. Caution should be
used when calibrating at temperatures greater than 35 °C,
however, because the commonly used nonlinear (International
Organization for Standardization, 1985) temperature compen-
sation deviates substantially when applied to KCl solutions.
Therefore, if a modern conductivity probe that expands
minimally with temperature is used and the temperature of the
water to be measured is between 5 and 35 °C, the temperature
of the calibration solutions (either laboratory or in situ) is not
expected to significantly affect the measurement of specific
conductance of samples in the field.
Conductivity standards are susceptible to contamination
or dilution from residual solutions, including rinse water, and
each calibration should be done with care. Low-conductivity
standards (<200 μS/cm) are especially susceptible to con-
tamination. Calibration in the laboratory prior to field
measurement, for both specific conductance meters and
multiparameter sondes, is recommended because the tempera-
ture is regulated and the standard solutions are less susceptible
to contamination.
Specific conductance meters must be checked or calibrated
before every field trip to ensure that the meter and probe
are working properly. The accuracy of specific conductance
measurements should be checked in the laboratory and in the
field by conducting measurements on standards whose specific
conductance is near the expected specific conductance of the
sample. Measurements of specific conductance standards that
are within ±5 μS/cm of the certified values for measurements
≤100 μS/cm or ±3 percent of the certified values for measure-
ments >100 μS/cm are considered accurate and do not warrant
recalibration. Because specific conductance meters typically
maintain their calibration, frequent recalibration is generally
```

</details>

<details><summary>[3] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.3/tm9-a6_3.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.3-specific-conductance.pdf__f9d002693709</summary>

```text
cted specific conductance of the
sample. Measurements of specific conductance standards that
are within ±5 μS/cm of the certified values for measurements
≤100 μS/cm or ±3 percent of the certified values for measure-
ments >100 μS/cm are considered accurate and do not warrant
recalibration. Because specific conductance meters typically
maintain their calibration, frequent recalibration is generallyunnecessary and may not improve data accuracy. If calibration
in the field is required, make certain the temperature of the
standards is stable and between 5 and 35 °C.
Specific conductance meters utilize either a one- or a
multipoint calibration procedure. Both calibration proce-
dures work well and the instruction manual accompanying
the specific conductance meter as well as the NFM should be
followed closely. Record and track the cell constant (one-point
calibration) or slope (multipoint calibration), as deviations can
be used to identify inaccurate calibrations or faulty equipment.
To verify that the specific conductance meter is responding
properly at low values of specific conductance, check the zero
response of the dry conductivity cell in air or DIW. The mea-
sured specific conductance of DIW should be <3 μS/cm.
Specific conductance meters are typically calibrated
with KCl solutions of known concentration that are traceable
to National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST).
Specific conductance standards with certificates of analyses
(NIST traceable) with values between 50 and 50,000 μS/cm
are available from USGS One Stop.1 Electrical conductivi-
ties of highly accurate KCl standards (0.01, 0.1, and 1.0 mole
per kilogram [mol/kg]) were determined by Pratt and others
(2001) and are shown in table 6.3–2. Extreme care should be
used when calibrating with dilute (<200-μS/cm) solutions,
as small amounts of contaminants or minor dilution can
substantially affect the calibration. Triple rinsing with DIW
and calibration standards is required. In addition to conduc-
tivity standards, the USGS Quality Systems Branch (QSB)
provides a variety of standard reference samples (SRSs),
prepared from natural waters, for quality-assurance testing and
measurements of specific conductance.
3.1 One-Point Calibration—Determination of
the Cell Constant
Many specific conductance meters and multiparameter
sondes utilize a one-point calibration for measurement of
specific conductance. Calibration of specific conductance
meters using a one-point calibration involves determination
of the cell constant (𝐾cell) by measuring the conductance (G)
of a standard solution with a known conductivity (κ) (see
equation 1, where the value of κ is known). Meters utiliz-
ing one-point calibrations typically require calibration with

the USGS One Stop ordering system (accessed September 11, 2017, at
https://1stop.usgs.gov/, internal USGS access only).
```

```text
standard solutions having specific conductance values of 500,
1,413, or 12,900 μS/cm. Select the standard with a specific
conductance that is closest to the expected specific conduc-
tance of the samples. Once the cell constant is determined,
the conductivity of the environmental water sample is deter-

determined by using equation 2. The cell constant of many
modern specific conductance probes is nearly constant (±2

because the physical dimension of the cell does not change
appreciably. Record and track the cell constant with each cali-
bration, as changes in the cell constant that exceed 2 percent
may be the result of a contaminated standard or dirty probe.
After the meter is calibrated, check the accuracy of the calibra-
tion by measuring at least one additional specific conductance
standard that brackets the expected range of environmental
values to be measured. Measurements of specific conductance
standards that are within ±5 μS/cm of the certified values for
measurements ≤100 μS/cm or ±3 percent of the certified val-
ues for measurements >100 μS/cm are considered accurate and
do not warrant recalibration.
3.2 Multipoint Calibration
Some specific conductance meters utilize a multipoint
calibration method. For a multipoint calibration, the conduc-
tance is determined for standards, and the conductivity of the
sample is determined by interpolation to a standard curve.
If the meter allows the user to select conductivity standards,
choose standards that bracket the expected specific conduc-
tance of the samples. Record and track the slope and intercept
with each calibration, as substantial changes may be the result
of contaminated standards or a dirty probe. Bias can occur
as a result of contamination as well as dilution from rinse
water. DIW and calibration standards with values less than
200 μS/cm are susceptible to bias from dilution or contamina-
tion and are difficult to measure accurately. Therefore, it is
recommended to calibrate specific conductance with stan-
dards with values that are greater than 200 μS/cm. After the
meter is calibrated, check the accuracy of the calibration by
measuring the specific conductance of at least one additional
standard near the expected range of environmental values to
```

```text
3.3 Meter Calibration Procedure
The calibration procedure described in this section
applies to most field specific conductance meters equipped
with a probe containing a dip-style conductivity cell and built-
in temperature compensation. Refer to the meter’s manual
for specific instructions. Calibration in the laboratory prior to
field measurement is recommended because the temperature
is regulated and the standard solutions are less susceptible to
contamination. If calibration in the field is required, the same
procedure should be followed. Make certain the temperature
of the standards is stable and between 5 and 35 °C.
Calibration in the laboratory prior to field measurement
is recommended because the temperature is regulated
and the standard solutions are less susceptible to
contamination.
```

```text
e Water
Measurements of specific conductance in flowing surface
water should represent the cross-sectional mean or median
specific conductance at the time of observation. Any deviation
from this convention must be documented in the database and
with the published data. This requirement is especially impor-
tant for poorly mixed streams at points downstream from a
convergence or source of drainage.• Before making a measurement of specific conduc-
tance—
• If you are unfamiliar with the sample site, measure
a depth and cross-sectional specific conductance
profile to determine the degree of system variability.
A dip-style conductivity probe works best for this
purpose.
• Refer to NFM A6.0 for information on which sam-
pling method to use.
• To measure specific conductance in surface water—
1. 	Check the accuracy of the specific conductance
measurement at the field site by measuring the
specific conductance of a check standard. Select
a check standard with a specific conductance
similar to that of the sample. The specific con-
ductance of check standards should be within
±5 μS/cm for measurements ≤100 μS/cm or ±3
percent for measurements >100 μS/cm. Recali-
brate if necessary.
2. 	Determine the variation of specific conductance
along a cross-sectional profile using an appropri-
ate measurement method (see NFM A6.0; use
equal-width increment [EWI] or equal-discharge
increment [EDI] method if required):
a. 	If the stream is flowing and wadable, wade
to the location(s) where specific conductance
is to be measured.
```

## probecal-ph-what-solutions#1 (probe-calibration, turn 1)

**Question:**

> The sheet that came with our pH probe says it is about due for a recalibration, but it never says how to do one. What do I actually dunk it in, and how many different solutions do I need?

**Must contain:**

1. States that at least two standard solutions are required, not one
2. States that a single-point calibration is not acceptable under standard USGS procedure
3. Names standard buffer values used for calibration, drawn from 1.68, 4.01, 7.00 and 10.01
4. States that the solutions chosen must bracket the pH expected in the water being measured
5. States that the buffers must be traceable to a NIST standard and must not be past their expiration date

**Must not:**

1. Tells the operator that a one-point calibration is sufficient
2. Invents buffer values that are not standard NIST-traceable buffers
3. Answers only with the datasheet's recalibration interval and supplies no procedure
4. Attributes the calibration procedure to a probe datasheet

**Answer (gpt-oss-120b, gold context):**

> To recalibrate the probe you need to use standard pH‑buffer solutions and follow a two‑ or three‑point calibration procedure.
> 
> * **What solutions to use** – The buffers are “ionic solutions that are used to calibrate the pH meter and electrodes”【1†"Standard pH buffer solutions (buffers) are ionic solutions that are used to calibrate the pH meter and electrodes."】. The commonly used ones are pH 1.68, 4.01, 7.00 and 10.01【2†"The commonly used standard pH buffer solutions and their corresponding pH values from 0 to 90 °C are shown in table 6.4–3."】.
> 
> * **How many different solutions** – USGS guidance requires “at least two pH buffer solutions … that bracket the sample pH for accurate calibration”【6†"Use at least two pH buffer solutions with documented, NIST-traceable pH values that bracket the sample pH for accurate calibration of the pH instrument system."】. A two‑point calibration is the minimum, and a three‑point calibration can be used for a larger range【4†"A two- or three-point calibration can be performed."】.
> 
> * **Choosing the pair** – If you expect the water pH to be between 7 and 8, use the pH‑7 and pH‑10.01 buffers【4†"If pH values are expected to be between 7 and 8, then the standard pH-7 and pH-10.01 buffers (at a minimum) should be selected."】. If you expect pH < 7, use the pH‑7 and pH‑4.01 buffers【4†"If pH values are expected to be less than 7, then the standard pH-7 and pH-4.01 buffers (at a minimum) should be selected."】. For the widest coverage you can use all three (pH 4.01, 7.00, 10.01)【4†"For most modern meters, a two-point calibration, typically with buffers selected from pH 10.01, 7.00, 4.01, or 1.68, is recommended."】.
> 
> * **Basic steps** – Rinse the electrode with de‑ionized water before each buffer, then immerse it in the buffer for the required time【7†"Note that the electrode and thermistor must be rinsed three times with DIW initially and between uses of each buffer."】. Follow the manufacturer’s order (often starting with pH 7) and record the temperature and buffer details as you calibrate【7†"Initiate the calibration process by following the manufacturer’s calibration instructions for the particular pH meter and electrode. Standard USGS procedure for calibration requires a two- or three-point calibration."】.
> 
> In short: use at least two of the standard buffers (pH 7 plus either 4.01 or 10.01, depending on expected water pH) and follow the rinse‑and‑immerse steps outlined above. If you want extra confidence, add the third buffer for a three‑point calibration.

**Correctness verdicts (DeepSeek judge):**

- Judge pass 1: **1** - The answer makes four of the five required points but omits the requirement that buffers must not be past their expiration date. Unmet: States that the buffers must be traceable to a NIST standard and must not be past their expiration date
- Judge pass 2: **1** - The answer makes points 1 3 and 4 but omits the explicit statement that a single-point calibration is not acceptable and does not mention expiration-date requirements so it is only partially complete. Unmet: States that a single-point calibration is not acceptable under standard USGS procedure; States that the buffers must be traceable to a NIST standard and must not be past their expiration date

**Excerpts the model was given (7), numbered as the model saw them:**

<details><summary>[1] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.4/tm9a6.4.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.4-ph.pdf__463363d709a0</summary>

```text
-- 14 of 32 --2.0 	Equipment and Supplies 	7
2.3 	Standard pH Buffer Solutions
Standard pH buffer solutions (buffers) are ionic solutions
that are used to calibrate the pH meter and electrodes. Buffers
maintain constant pH values (at a single temperature) because
of their ability to resist changes to the specific pH value for
which they are produced. However, the pH of solutions is
affected by temperature, because the calibration slope (which
is related to the Nernst equation) and the isopotential point
change with temperature. Modern pH meters are typically
calibrated with pH buffers having well-known pH-temper-
ature correlations; quality-assured and National Institute of
Standards and Technology- (NIST) traceable pH buffers are
available from USGS One-Stop1. Most pH meters recognize
the e.m.f. range of certain pH buffers (commonly pH 1.68,
4.01, 7.00, and 10.01 buffers) and are able to automatically
compensate for temperature during calibration. Verify that
the buffers you are using are NIST-traceable, that the values
are recognized correctly by the meter, and that the buffers are
compatible with the ATC for your meter. Consult the manu-
facturer’s manual to be sure that the meter will adjust the
expected value of the buffer for the temperature measured. The
user may be responsible for entering the temperature-adjusted
buffer pH value into the meter for calibration from a chart or
the meter may automatically generate a temperature-adjusted
pH value for the buffer.
Measurements of pH are only as accurate as the buffers
used to calibrate the meter and electrodes.
The commonly used pH buffer solutions and their corre-
sponding pH values from 0 to 90 °C are shown in table 6.4–3.
The pH buffers 1.68, 4.01, and 10.01 are primary standards
(pH values determined by using the standard hydrogen-gas
electrode). Their compositions are typically similar for various
manufacturers; therefore, their variations in pH with tem-
perature are well known. The pH-7.00 buffer is a secondary
standard (pH values determined by interpolation to primary
standards) and its composition (and therefore pH dependence
on temperature) may differ with the manufacturer. However,
the pH-7.00 buffer is typically made either from potassium
dihydrogen phosphate plus sodium hydroxide or from potas-
sium dihydrogen phosphate plus sodium hydrogen phosphate;
the pH values of both buffer formulations are comparable to
within 0.02 pH units from 0 to 90 °C.
Use the following precautions and protocols to ensure the
accuracy of the pH buffers—(modified from Busenberg and
Plummer, 1987):
1Equipment and supplies that are tested and quality assured are avail-
able to USGS field studies from the National Field Supply Service (NFSS)
and the Hydrologic Instrumentation Facility (HIF), and are sold through
the USGS One Stop ordering system (accessed February 5, 2020, at
https://1stop.usgs.gov/) (internal USGS access only).
• Use only buffers that have been certified traceable to a
NIST standard reference material.
• Label pH buffer containers with the date opened and
the expiration date. Copy the expiration date and the
buffer lot number onto any reagent containers into
```

</details>

```text
y (HIF), and are sold through
the USGS One Stop ordering system (accessed February 5, 2020, at
https://1stop.usgs.gov/) (internal USGS access only).
• Use only buffers that have been certified traceable to a
NIST standard reference material.
• Label pH buffer containers with the date opened and
the expiration date. Copy the expiration date and the
buffer lot number onto any reagent containers intowhich the buffer is transferred. This information is also
recorded in the pH-meter/electrode logbook.
• Do not use expired buffers.
• Cap buffer bottles firmly after use to prevent
evaporation and contamination.
• Never pour used buffer back into a bottle containing the
stock buffer solution.
```

```text
, do not
store the electrode in buffer solution for a long period.
• Gel-filled electrodes should be stored according to the

General guidelines for long-term storage:
• Store electrodes in the appropriate storage solu-
tion and cap as recommended by the manufacturer.
For long-term storage, wrap the electrode cap and
filling-hole plug with lab plastic wrap (Parafilm) toprevent formation of excess salts or evaporation of
the reference solution. Liquid-filled electrodes may

• Clean the electrode cable contacts and connector

• Store every component of the pH-measuring system in

hot or cold temperatures.
4.0 	Calibration of the pH
Instrument System
Proper calibration of the pH instrument system is crucial
to accurate measurement of the pH of environmental samples.
A calibration curve is created by measurement of two or
more standard pH buffers that bracket the pH of the water
being measured. The ideal slope of the calibration curve
at 25 °C, based on the Nernst equation (see section 2.2), is
59.16 mV/pH unit. Almost all pH electrodes are designed to
```

</details>

<details><summary>[4] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.4/tm9a6.4.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.4-ph.pdf__b1653e60dd01</summary>

```text
-- 18 of 32 --4.0 	Calibration of the pH Instrument System 	11
have an isopotential point at pH 7 (the pH at which e.m.f. is
equal to 0 mV). Most modern pH meters are equipped with
ATC, which compensates for the temperature dependence of
the standard buffers. The Nernst equation gives the expected
(theoretical) response potential of the pH buffer at the specific
temperature of the calibration (Hem, 1989), and after calibra-
tion most meters report the calibration slope as a percentage
of the Nernstian theoretical slope. To successfully calibrate
pH instrumentation, (1) the meter and pH electrode must be
functioning properly, (2) the system must be calibrated with
pH buffers so the pH of the solution being measured is within
the calibrated range, and (3) the temperature of the pH buffers
during calibration needs be stable and similar to (within 10 °C
of) the temperature of the solution being measured.
A properly working pH system has—
• new or adequately charged batteries
• a thermistor that functions properly and accurately
measures the temperature
• a pH electrode with an accurate Nernstian response
(slope of 95–101 percent of the theoretical
Nernstian slope)
• an isopotential point near pH 7
• a quick response time (1–2 minutes) in standard
buffer solutions.
Accurate measurement of the pH of environmental
samples requires calibration with pH buffers that bracket the
pH of the sample. Before the pH instrument system is cali-
brated, it is useful to estimate (or anticipate from historical
site data, if available) the pH and conductivity of the waters to
be encountered at the field sites. If no data are available from
which to estimate sample pH, then pH indicator paper can
be used onsite as a gross indicator of the pH of the site, but
under no circumstances should a pH value from indica-
tor paper be recorded as the site pH. A two- or three-point
calibration can be performed. Most manufacturers use a linear
calibration curve for all calibrations (including multipoint
calibrations). For most modern meters, a two-point calibration,
typically with buffers selected from pH 10.01, 7.00, 4.01, or
1.68, is recommended. pH meters that use either a point-to-
point calibration or a nonlinear function are not recommended.
Follow the manufacturer’s instructions with respect to recom-
mended buffers and the sequence of buffer calibration.
Examples: When measuring pH in a stream—
• If pH values are expected to be between 7 and 8,
then the standard pH-7 and pH-10.01 buffers (at a
minimum) should be selected.
• If pH values are expected to be less than 7, then the
standard pH-7 and pH-4.01 buffers (at a minimum)
should be selected.
For the two examples above, a three-point calibration
using pH-4.01, -7.00, and -10.01 buffers could also be used to
allow measurement of a larger range of pH.
The pH of a solution changes with temperature, and most
modern pH meters automatically compensate for temperature
differences between the buffers used for calibration and the
measurements made in samples. However, electrodes may not
behave ideally and this feature should be tested regularly. Cali-
brating a pH meter and electrode with buffers that are within
```

</details>

<details><summary>[5] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.4/tm9a6.4.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.4-ph.pdf__ff61308669d5</summary>

```text
ow measurement of a larger range of pH.
The pH of a solution changes with temperature, and most
modern pH meters automatically compensate for temperature
differences between the buffers used for calibration and the
measurements made in samples. However, electrodes may not
behave ideally and this feature should be tested regularly. Cali-
brating a pH meter and electrode with buffers that are within±10 °C of the sample temperature is recommended. This task
can be accomplished by placing the buffers in a mesh bag and
equilibrating them to the temperature of the water at the sam-
pling location before calibration. However, for most sampling
situations, where the temperature of the water is between 5 and
35 °C and the pH is between 4.0 and 9.5, the recommended
procedure is to calibrate the meter in the more controlled
environment of the office laboratory, and then allow the meter
to compensate for temperature differences at the sampling site.
Again, the accuracy of field measurements should be checked
regularly with buffers equilibrated to the environmental-
sample temperatures to verify the readings. If the pH of the
buffers checked at the field site at ±10 °C of the temperature of
the environmental sample is more than ±0.05 pH unit different
from the value the buffer should return at that temperature, the
meter and electrode should be recalibrated at a temperature
that is ±10 °C of the field-measurement temperature.
For multiparameter sondes that will be deployed for
continuous measurements in the field in a range of conditions,
the recommended procedure is to calibrate in the laboratory
and then conduct checks in the field at ambient tempera-
tures to verify the accuracy of the readings. Recalibration
may be necessary if the initial check is more than ±0.05 pH
unit different from the value the buffer should return. If the
measurement temperature is more than ±10 °C different from
the laboratory calibration temperature, checks in the field are
more likely to fail and recalibration may be necessary.
The following guidelines and standard procedures
generally apply whenever a pH instrument system is to be
calibrated. Because calibration and operating procedures can
differ with the instrument system, check the manufacturer’s
recommended calibration procedures and calibration buffer
solution requirements. Modern pH meters typically compen-
sate for buffer temperatures automatically during calibration.
When using these instruments, follow the manufacturer’s
calibration instructions and the more general instructions in
the following procedures—do not take shortcuts.
• Before each field trip and site visit, check pH-meter/
electrode logbook records for electrode performance.
Remember—any noted calibration slope of less
than 95 percent or greater than 101 percent indicates
probable electrode deterioration. If the slope is outside
the acceptable range (95–101 percent), the electrode
should be cleaned, reconditioned, or replaced, and the
new electrode calibrated. If a liquid-filled electrode is
being used, try reconditioning it (section 3.2) in the
laboratory before replacing it with a new electrode.
```

```text
-- 19 of 32 --12 	Chapter A6.4. Measurement of pH
• Use at least two pH buffer solutions with documented,
NIST-traceable pH values that bracket the sample pH
for accurate calibration of the pH instrument system.
• If a large primary source of pH buffer is purchased
initially, use a secondary bottle to store pH buffers
while in the field to minimize the chance of contami-
nating the main pH buffer source. Pour the amount of
each buffer needed from the source container into a
clean polyethylene bottle dedicated for the respective
buffer, and label the bottle with the buffer’s pH value,
lot number, and expiration date. Keep a printed chart
of the temperature-adjusted pH values provided by the
manufacturer for that buffer.
• A calibration check of the pH meter’s temperature
sensor is required to be performed at least annually
(NFM A6.1). Most single-analyte pH meters use a
thermistor built into the electrode. Multiparameter
instruments typically have the temperature sensor
in a different part of the instrument system than the
pH electrode.
• The pH instrumentation should be calibrated daily
and recalibrated if a check of either of the two pH
buffers nearest to the pH of the sample at a temperature
that is ± 10 °C of the sample deviates by more than
± 0.05 pH units.
• The isopotential point is the characteristic pH where
the e.m.f. is equal to 0 mV. Most modern pH electrodes
are designed to have an isopotential point at or near
pH=7. Test the electrode when it is new by placing it
in pH-7.00 buffer and record the voltage. Repeat this
test regularly and verify that the value is ±15 mV of
the original value. If the isopotential point is more than
±15 mV of the original value, clean, recondition, or
replace the electrode.
Do not use pH buffers that have exceeded their date of
expiration. Buffers that have been transferred to a second-
ary bottle for use while in the field should be refreshed
before every field trip.
4.1 	Calibration Procedure
The following calibration procedure is expected to yield
accurate sample-pH measurements for waters with specific
conductance less than 60,000 μS/cm, temperatures from 0 to
90 °C, and pH in the range from 1.68 to 10. Standard buffer
solutions and combination pH electrode can be appropri-
ately used to achieve an accurate measurement of the pH
of environmental waters that is within these environmental
ranges. Studies of waters with conditions that fall outside
the ranges described above may require pH calibration and
measurement modifications.
4.1.1 	Calibration of a Single-Parameter Meter
and pH Electrode used for Sample Measurement
or an Alkalinity Titration
1. 	Under most situations, it is recommended to calibrate
the pH meter and electrode in a controlled environment
such as a laboratory rather than in the field. Most mod-
ern meters automatically adjust pH measurements for
changes in temperature, and these corrections are usually
accurate within a temperature range of 5 to 35 °C and for
pH measurements between 4.0 and 9.5. Electrodes may
not behave ideally, however, and this feature should be
tested regularly with measurements on buffers equili-
brated to ambient field temperatures.
```

```text
the electrode body but does not cover the fill hole.
The fill hole should be uncovered and open to the air.
Immerse the electrode in the buffer solution; swirl

reference junction to the bottom of the bulb. Discard
the buffer appropriately. Repeat this step for a total
of two times.

aliquot of buffer into the beaker and immerse the
electrode in the buffer for 1 minute. Discard the buf-
fer appropriately.
i. 	Pour sit measure—Pour fresh buffer into the beaker.
Immerse the electrode, gently swirl to remove all
air bubbles surrounding it, and allow it to sit for 1
```

## probecal-ph-what-solutions#2 (probe-calibration, turn 2)

**Earlier in this conversation (turn 1 question):**

> The sheet that came with our pH probe says it is about due for a recalibration, but it never says how to do one. What do I actually dunk it in, and how many different solutions do I need?

**Turn 1 answer:**

> To recalibrate the probe you need to use standard pH‑buffer solutions and follow a two‑ or three‑point calibration procedure.
> 
> * **What solutions to use** – The buffers are “ionic solutions that are used to calibrate the pH meter and electrodes”【1†"Standard pH buffer solutions (buffers) are ionic solutions that are used to calibrate the pH meter and electrodes."】. The commonly used ones are pH 1.68, 4.01, 7.00 and 10.01【2†"The commonly used standard pH buffer solutions and their corresponding pH values from 0 to 90 °C are shown in table 6.4–3."】.
> 
> * **How many different solutions** – USGS guidance requires “at least two pH buffer solutions … that bracket the sample pH for accurate calibration”【6†"Use at least two pH buffer solutions with documented, NIST-traceable pH values that bracket the sample pH for accurate calibration of the pH instrument system."】. A two‑point calibration is the minimum, and a three‑point calibration can be used for a larger range【4†"A two- or three-point calibration can be performed."】.
> 
> * **Choosing the pair** – If you expect the water pH to be between 7 and 8, use the pH‑7 and pH‑10.01 buffers【4†"If pH values are expected to be between 7 and 8, then the standard pH-7 and pH-10.01 buffers (at a minimum) should be selected."】. If you expect pH < 7, use the pH‑7 and pH‑4.01 buffers【4†"If pH values are expected to be less than 7, then the standard pH-7 and pH-4.01 buffers (at a minimum) should be selected."】. For the widest coverage you can use all three (pH 4.01, 7.00, 10.01)【4†"For most modern meters, a two-point calibration, typically with buffers selected from pH 10.01, 7.00, 4.01, or 1.68, is recommended."】.
> 
> * **Basic steps** – Rinse the electrode with de‑ionized water before each buffer, then immerse it in the buffer for the required time【7†"Note that the electrode and thermistor must be rinsed three times with DIW initially and between uses of each buffer."】. Follow the manufacturer’s order (often starting with pH 7) and record the temperature and buffer details as you calibrate【7†"Initiate the calibration process by following the manufacturer’s calibration instructions for the particular pH meter and electrode. Standard USGS procedure for calibration requires a two- or three-point calibration."】.
> 
> In short: use at least two of the standard buffers (pH 7 plus either 4.01 or 10.01, depending on expected water pH) and follow the rinse‑and‑immerse steps outlined above. If you want extra confidence, add the third buffer for a three‑point calibration.

**Question:**

> Our creek sits around 7.5 most of the year. Which of those should I pick, and does it matter how cold the bottles are when I pour them out?

**Must contain:**

1. Selects the pH 7 buffer together with the pH 10.01 buffer for water expected between 7 and 8
2. States that calibration begins with the pH 7 buffer when the meter does not dictate an order
3. States that buffer temperature should be within about 10 degrees Celsius of the sample temperature
4. States that a buffer's pH value shifts with temperature, so the temperature-corrected value must be used rather than the nominal value

**Must not:**

1. Treats the nominal 25 degree Celsius label value as correct at any temperature
2. Picks a bracket that does not span the expected sample pH, for example 4.01 and 7.00 for water near pH 7.5
3. Invents a temperature tolerance the manual does not state

**Answer (gpt-oss-120b, gold context):**

> For a creek that stays near pH 7.5, the USGS guidance says you should use the pH‑7.00 buffer together with the pH‑10.01 buffer as the minimum pair【4†"If pH values are expected to be between 7 and 8, then the standard pH-7 and pH-10.01 buffers (at a minimum) should be selected."】. Adding the pH‑4.01 buffer would give a three‑point calibration, but it isn’t required for this range.
> 
> **Does the temperature of the buffer bottles matter?**  
> Yes. The pH of a buffer changes with temperature, and although most modern meters automatically compensate for temperature differences, the calibration is most reliable when the buffers are at a temperature close to that of the water being measured【4†"The pH of a solution changes with temperature, and most modern pH meters automatically compensate for temperature differences between the buffers used for calibration and the measurements made in samples."】. USGS recommends using buffers that are within ± 10 °C of the sample temperature【5†"Calibrating a pH meter and electrode with buffers that are within±10 °C of the sample temperature is recommended."】. If the bottles are much colder (or warmer) than the creek water, let the buffers equilibrate to the creek temperature—or at least keep them within that ±10 °C window—before performing the calibration. This helps ensure the calibration check stays within the ±0.05 pH‑unit tolerance that triggers a recalibration.

**Correctness verdicts (DeepSeek judge):**

- Judge pass 1: **1** - The answer correctly selects the pH 7 and 10.01 buffers and states the ±10 °C temperature guideline but it does not state that calibration begins with pH 7 when no order is specified nor does it explicitly state that the temperature-corrected buffer value must be used rather than the nominal value. Unmet: States calibration begins with pH 7 when meter does not dictate order; States buffer pH shifts with temperature so temperature-corrected value must be used rather than nominal
- Judge pass 2: **1** - The answer correctly selects the pH 7 and 10.01 buffers and states the ±10 °C temperature guideline but does not state that calibration begins with pH 7 when no order is given nor that the temperature-corrected buffer value must be used instead of the nominal value. Unmet: States calibration begins with pH 7 when meter does not dictate order; States buffer pH shifts with temperature so temperature-corrected value must be used

<details><summary>[1] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.4/tm9a6.4.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.4-ph.pdf__463363d709a0</summary>

```text
-- 14 of 32 --2.0 	Equipment and Supplies 	7
2.3 	Standard pH Buffer Solutions
Standard pH buffer solutions (buffers) are ionic solutions
that are used to calibrate the pH meter and electrodes. Buffers
maintain constant pH values (at a single temperature) because
of their ability to resist changes to the specific pH value for
which they are produced. However, the pH of solutions is
affected by temperature, because the calibration slope (which
is related to the Nernst equation) and the isopotential point
change with temperature. Modern pH meters are typically
calibrated with pH buffers having well-known pH-temper-
ature correlations; quality-assured and National Institute of
Standards and Technology- (NIST) traceable pH buffers are
available from USGS One-Stop1. Most pH meters recognize
the e.m.f. range of certain pH buffers (commonly pH 1.68,
4.01, 7.00, and 10.01 buffers) and are able to automatically
compensate for temperature during calibration. Verify that
the buffers you are using are NIST-traceable, that the values
are recognized correctly by the meter, and that the buffers are
compatible with the ATC for your meter. Consult the manu-
facturer’s manual to be sure that the meter will adjust the
expected value of the buffer for the temperature measured. The
user may be responsible for entering the temperature-adjusted
buffer pH value into the meter for calibration from a chart or
the meter may automatically generate a temperature-adjusted
pH value for the buffer.
Measurements of pH are only as accurate as the buffers
used to calibrate the meter and electrodes.
The commonly used pH buffer solutions and their corre-
sponding pH values from 0 to 90 °C are shown in table 6.4–3.
The pH buffers 1.68, 4.01, and 10.01 are primary standards
(pH values determined by using the standard hydrogen-gas
electrode). Their compositions are typically similar for various
manufacturers; therefore, their variations in pH with tem-
perature are well known. The pH-7.00 buffer is a secondary
standard (pH values determined by interpolation to primary
standards) and its composition (and therefore pH dependence
on temperature) may differ with the manufacturer. However,
the pH-7.00 buffer is typically made either from potassium
dihydrogen phosphate plus sodium hydroxide or from potas-
sium dihydrogen phosphate plus sodium hydrogen phosphate;
the pH values of both buffer formulations are comparable to
within 0.02 pH units from 0 to 90 °C.
Use the following precautions and protocols to ensure the
accuracy of the pH buffers—(modified from Busenberg and
Plummer, 1987):
1Equipment and supplies that are tested and quality assured are avail-
able to USGS field studies from the National Field Supply Service (NFSS)
and the Hydrologic Instrumentation Facility (HIF), and are sold through
the USGS One Stop ordering system (accessed February 5, 2020, at
https://1stop.usgs.gov/) (internal USGS access only).
• Use only buffers that have been certified traceable to a
NIST standard reference material.
• Label pH buffer containers with the date opened and
the expiration date. Copy the expiration date and the
buffer lot number onto any reagent containers into
```

```text
4.0 	Calibration of the pH
Instrument System
Proper calibration of the pH instrument system is crucial
to accurate measurement of the pH of environmental samples.
A calibration curve is created by measurement of two or
more standard pH buffers that bracket the pH of the water
being measured. The ideal slope of the calibration curve
at 25 °C, based on the Nernst equation (see section 2.2), is
59.16 mV/pH unit. Almost all pH electrodes are designed to
```

<details><summary>[4] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.4/tm9a6.4.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.4-ph.pdf__b1653e60dd01</summary>

```text
-- 18 of 32 --4.0 	Calibration of the pH Instrument System 	11
have an isopotential point at pH 7 (the pH at which e.m.f. is
equal to 0 mV). Most modern pH meters are equipped with
ATC, which compensates for the temperature dependence of
the standard buffers. The Nernst equation gives the expected
(theoretical) response potential of the pH buffer at the specific
temperature of the calibration (Hem, 1989), and after calibra-
tion most meters report the calibration slope as a percentage
of the Nernstian theoretical slope. To successfully calibrate
pH instrumentation, (1) the meter and pH electrode must be
functioning properly, (2) the system must be calibrated with
pH buffers so the pH of the solution being measured is within
the calibrated range, and (3) the temperature of the pH buffers
during calibration needs be stable and similar to (within 10 °C
of) the temperature of the solution being measured.
A properly working pH system has—
• new or adequately charged batteries
• a thermistor that functions properly and accurately
measures the temperature
• a pH electrode with an accurate Nernstian response
(slope of 95–101 percent of the theoretical
Nernstian slope)
• an isopotential point near pH 7
• a quick response time (1–2 minutes) in standard
buffer solutions.
Accurate measurement of the pH of environmental
samples requires calibration with pH buffers that bracket the
pH of the sample. Before the pH instrument system is cali-
brated, it is useful to estimate (or anticipate from historical
site data, if available) the pH and conductivity of the waters to
be encountered at the field sites. If no data are available from
which to estimate sample pH, then pH indicator paper can
be used onsite as a gross indicator of the pH of the site, but
under no circumstances should a pH value from indica-
tor paper be recorded as the site pH. A two- or three-point
calibration can be performed. Most manufacturers use a linear
calibration curve for all calibrations (including multipoint
calibrations). For most modern meters, a two-point calibration,
typically with buffers selected from pH 10.01, 7.00, 4.01, or
1.68, is recommended. pH meters that use either a point-to-
point calibration or a nonlinear function are not recommended.
Follow the manufacturer’s instructions with respect to recom-
mended buffers and the sequence of buffer calibration.
Examples: When measuring pH in a stream—
• If pH values are expected to be between 7 and 8,
then the standard pH-7 and pH-10.01 buffers (at a
minimum) should be selected.
• If pH values are expected to be less than 7, then the
standard pH-7 and pH-4.01 buffers (at a minimum)
should be selected.
For the two examples above, a three-point calibration
using pH-4.01, -7.00, and -10.01 buffers could also be used to
allow measurement of a larger range of pH.
The pH of a solution changes with temperature, and most
modern pH meters automatically compensate for temperature
differences between the buffers used for calibration and the
measurements made in samples. However, electrodes may not
behave ideally and this feature should be tested regularly. Cali-
brating a pH meter and electrode with buffers that are within
```

</details>

<details><summary>[5] https://pubs.usgs.gov/tm/09/a6.4/tm9a6.4.pdf - usgs-nfm-a6.4-ph.pdf__ff61308669d5</summary>

```text
ow measurement of a larger range of pH.
The pH of a solution changes with temperature, and most
modern pH meters automatically compensate for temperature
differences between the buffers used for calibration and the
measurements made in samples. However, electrodes may not
behave ideally and this feature should be tested regularly. Cali-
brating a pH meter and electrode with buffers that are within±10 °C of the sample temperature is recommended. This task
can be accomplished by placing the buffers in a mesh bag and
equilibrating them to the temperature of the water at the sam-
pling location before calibration. However, for most sampling
situations, where the temperature of the water is between 5 and
35 °C and the pH is between 4.0 and 9.5, the recommended
procedure is to calibrate the meter in the more controlled
environment of the office laboratory, and then allow the meter
to compensate for temperature differences at the sampling site.
Again, the accuracy of field measurements should be checked
regularly with buffers equilibrated to the environmental-
sample temperatures to verify the readings. If the pH of the
buffers checked at the field site at ±10 °C of the temperature of
the environmental sample is more than ±0.05 pH unit different
from the value the buffer should return at that temperature, the
meter and electrode should be recalibrated at a temperature
that is ±10 °C of the field-measurement temperature.
For multiparameter sondes that will be deployed for
continuous measurements in the field in a range of conditions,
the recommended procedure is to calibrate in the laboratory
and then conduct checks in the field at ambient tempera-
tures to verify the accuracy of the readings. Recalibration
may be necessary if the initial check is more than ±0.05 pH
unit different from the value the buffer should return. If the
measurement temperature is more than ±10 °C different from
the laboratory calibration temperature, checks in the field are
more likely to fail and recalibration may be necessary.
The following guidelines and standard procedures
generally apply whenever a pH instrument system is to be
calibrated. Because calibration and operating procedures can
differ with the instrument system, check the manufacturer’s
recommended calibration procedures and calibration buffer
solution requirements. Modern pH meters typically compen-
sate for buffer temperatures automatically during calibration.
When using these instruments, follow the manufacturer’s
calibration instructions and the more general instructions in
the following procedures—do not take shortcuts.
• Before each field trip and site visit, check pH-meter/
electrode logbook records for electrode performance.
Remember—any noted calibration slope of less
than 95 percent or greater than 101 percent indicates
probable electrode deterioration. If the slope is outside
the acceptable range (95–101 percent), the electrode
should be cleaned, reconditioned, or replaced, and the
new electrode calibrated. If a liquid-filled electrode is
being used, try reconditioning it (section 3.2) in the
laboratory before replacing it with a new electrode.
```

```text
6. 	Turn on the pH meter. Note any error messages
displayed by the meter during startup and record them
in the pH-meter/electrode logbook. Correct any error
messages. Malfunctioning meters may require manufac-
turer attention.
7. 	Record in the pH-meter/electrode logbook any impor-
tant or unusual startup information displayed by the pH
meter. A calibration log may be paper or electronic, but
is required for all calibrations and is helpful in tracking
the performance of the electrode over time.
8. 	Initiate the calibration process by following the manu-
facturer’s calibration instructions for the particular pH
meter and electrode. Standard USGS procedure for
calibration requires a two- or three-point calibration.
• Some pH instrument systems may use a different
multipoint calibration procedure; in such
cases, follow the instructions provided in the
instrument manual.
• A single-point calibration, recommended by some
manufacturers, is not acceptable for USGS field
measurement of pH.
USGS procedure for calibration of pH requires a two- or
three-point calibration.
9. 	Record the temperature and value of pH buffers used at
that temperature, with lot numbers and expiration dates,
in the pH-meter/electrode logbook.
10. 	Begin calibration procedures:
a. 	Note that the electrode and thermistor must be rinsed
three times with DIW initially and between uses of
each buffer.
b. 	If the meter does not specify a calibration order,
begin with pH 7.
c. 	To minimize contamination, each pH buffer should
be stored in a dedicated, capped bottle that is
refreshed with buffer before each field trip. The
bottles should be labeled with the buffer value, lot
number, and expiration date.
d. 	The calibration of the electrode should be performed
in a small plastic or glass beaker that is dedicated to
the pH calibration.
e. 	Rinse the electrode and thermistor with DIW by
pouring the DIW directly over the electrode. Rinse the
calibration beaker with DIW three times.
f. 	Rinse the electrode and thermistor with the first buf-
fer by pouring the buffer directly over the electrode
and discarding the solution to waste.
g. 	First and second rinse, pour swirl discard—Pour
enough buffer into the dedicated beaker so that it
covers the electrode reference junction and some of
the electrode body but does not cover the fill hole.
The fill hole should be uncovered and open to the air.
Immerse the electrode in the buffer solution; swirl
the buffer to rinse the electrode body from above the
reference junction to the bottom of the bulb. Discard
the buffer appropriately. Repeat this step for a total
of two times.
h. 	Third rinse, pour sit discard—Pour the next
aliquot of buffer into the beaker and immerse the
electrode in the buffer for 1 minute. Discard the buf-
fer appropriately.
i. 	Pour sit measure—Pour fresh buffer into the beaker.
Immerse the electrode, gently swirl to remove all
air bubbles surrounding it, and allow it to sit for 1
minute.
```
